<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:142px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Benedictxvhandwriting.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Benedictxvhandwriting.jpg/140px-Benedictxvhandwriting.jpg" decoding="async" width="140" height="107" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Benedictxvhandwriting.jpg/210px-Benedictxvhandwriting.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Benedictxvhandwriting.jpg/280px-Benedictxvhandwriting.jpg 2x" data-file-width="999" data-file-height="761" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Benedictxvhandwriting.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The handwriting of Pope Benedict XV</div></div></div>
<p>In internal Church affairs, Benedict XV reiterated Pius X's condemnation of <a href="/wiki/Modernism_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Modernism in the Catholic Church">Modernist scholars</a> and the errors in modern philosophical systems in <i><a href="/wiki/Ad_beatissimi_Apostolorum" title="Ad beatissimi Apostolorum">Ad beatissimi Apostolorum</a></i>. He declined to readmit to communion scholars who had been excommunicated during the previous pontificate. However, he calmed what he saw as the excesses of the anti-Modernist campaign within the Church. On 25 July 1920, he wrote the <a href="/wiki/Motu_proprio" title="Motu proprio">motu proprio</a> <a href="/wiki/Bonum_sane" title="Bonum sane">Bonum sane</a> on <a href="/wiki/Saint_Joseph" title="Saint Joseph">Saint Joseph</a> and against naturalism and socialism.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Canon_law_reform">Canon law reform</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Canon law reform">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1917_Code_of_Canon_Law" title="1917 Code of Canon Law">1917 Code of Canon Law</a></div>
<p>In 1917 Benedict XV promulgated the Church's first comprehensive <a href="/wiki/1917_Code_of_Canon_Law" title="1917 Code of Canon Law">Code of Canon Law</a>, the preparation of which had been commissioned by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_X" title="Pope Pius X">Pope Pius X</a>, and which is thus known as the Pio-Benedictine Code. This Code, which entered into force in 1918, was the first consolidation of the Church's <a href="/wiki/Canon_law_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon law (Catholic Church)">Canon Law</a> into a modern Code made up of simple articles. Previously, Canon Law was dispersed in a variety of sources and partial compilations. The new codification of canon law is credited with reviving religious life and providing judicial clarity throughout the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-Franzen_382_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Franzen_382-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In addition, continuing the concerns of <a href="/wiki/Leo_XIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Leo XIII">Leo XIII</a>, he furthered Eastern Catholic culture, theology and liturgy by founding an Oriental Institute for them in Rome<sup id="cite_ref-Franzen_382_6-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Franzen_382-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and creating the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Eastern_Churches" class="mw-redirect" title="Congregation for the Eastern Churches">Sacred Congregation for the Oriental Church</a> in 1917.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Catholic_missions">Catholic missions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Catholic missions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 30 November 1919, Benedict XV appealed to all Catholics worldwide to sacrifice for Catholic missions, stating at the same time in <i><a href="/wiki/Maximum_illud" title="Maximum illud">Maximum illud</a></i> that these missions should foster local culture and not import European cultures.<sup id="cite_ref-Franzen_382_6-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Franzen_382-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The damages of such cultural imports<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> were particularly grave in Africa and Asia, where many missionaries were deported and incarcerated if they happened to originate from a hostile nation.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mariology">Mariology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Mariology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:SalusPopuliromanicrowned.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/SalusPopuliromanicrowned.jpg/170px-SalusPopuliromanicrowned.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="252" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/SalusPopuliromanicrowned.jpg/255px-SalusPopuliromanicrowned.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/SalusPopuliromanicrowned.jpg/340px-SalusPopuliromanicrowned.jpg 2x" data-file-width="753" data-file-height="1118" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:SalusPopuliromanicrowned.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>"<i><a href="/wiki/Salus_Populi_Romani" title="Salus Populi Romani">Salus Populi Romani</a></i>"</div></div></div>
<p>Pope Benedict personally addressed in numerous letters the pilgrims at Marian sanctuaries. He named Mary the Patron of Bavaria, and permitted, in Mexico, the Feast of the Immaculate Conception of Guadaloupe. He authorised the Feast of Mary Mediator of all Graces.<sup id="cite_ref-AAS_1921,_345_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AAS_1921,_345-7">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He condemned the misuse of Marian statues and pictures, dressed in priestly robes, which he outlawed 4 April 1916.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 10 May 1916, Pope Benedict declared the image and Marian title of Our Lady of Charity of El Cobre as Patroness of Cuba at the written request of the soldier veterans of the Cuban War of Independence.
</p><p>During <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, Benedict placed the world under the protection of the <a href="/wiki/Mary,_mother_of_Jesus" title="Mary, mother of Jesus">Blessed Virgin Mary</a> and added the invocation <i>Mary Queen of Peace</i> to the <a href="/wiki/Litany_of_Loreto" class="mw-redirect" title="Litany of Loreto">Litany of Loreto</a>. He promoted <a href="/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Veneration of Mary in the Catholic Church">Marian veneration</a> throughout the world by elevating 20 well-known Marian shrines such as <a href="/wiki/Ettal_Abbey" title="Ettal Abbey">Ettal Abbey</a> in Bavaria into <a href="/wiki/Minor_basilica" class="mw-redirect" title="Minor basilica">minor basilicas</a>. He also promoted Marian devotions in May.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The dogmatic constitution on the Church issued by the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a> quotes the Marian theology of Benedict XV.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Benedict issued the motu proprio <i>Bonum sane</i> on 25 July 1920, encouraging devotion to <a href="/wiki/Saint_Joseph" title="Saint Joseph">Saint Joseph</a> "since through St. Joseph we go directly to Mary, and through Mary to the source of every holiness, Jesus Christ, who consecrated the domestic virtues with his obedience to St. Joseph and Mary."<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He issued an encyclical on <a href="/wiki/Ephraim_the_Syrian" class="mw-redirect" title="Ephraim the Syrian">Ephraim the Syrian</a> depicting Ephraim as a model of Marian devotion, as well as the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Letter" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic Letter">Apostolic Letter</a> <i>Inter Soldalica</i> of 22 March 1918.<sup id="cite_ref-AAS,_1918,_181_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AAS,_1918,_181-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>"As the blessed Virgin Mary does not seem to participate in the public life of Jesus Christ, and then, suddenly appears at the stations of his cross, she is not there without divine intention. She suffers with her suffering and dying son, almost as if she would have died herself. For the salvation of mankind, she gave up her rights as the mother of her son and sacrificed him for the reconciliation of divine justice, <i>as far as she was permitted to do</i>. Therefore, <i>one can say</i>, she redeemed with Christ the human race."<sup id="cite_ref-AAS,_1918,_181_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AAS,_1918,_181-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>During his seven-year pontificate, Benedict XV wrote a total of twelve <a href="/wiki/Encyclical" title="Encyclical">encyclicals</a>. In addition to the encyclicals mentioned, he issued <i>In hac tanta</i> on St. Boniface (14 May 1919), <i>Paterno iam diu</i> on the Children of Central Europe (24 November 1919), <i>Spiritus Paraclitus</i> on St. Jerome (September 1920), <i>Principi Apostolorum Petro</i> on St. Ephram the Syrian (5 October 1920), <i>Annus iam plenus</i> also on Children in Central Europe (1 December 1920), <i>Sacra propediem</i> on the Third Order of St. Francis (6 January 1921), <i><a href="/wiki/In_praeclara_summorum" title="In praeclara summorum">In praeclara summorum</a></i> on Dante (30 April 1921), and <i>Fausto appetente die</i> on St. Dominic (29 June 1921).
</p><p>His <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Exhortation" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic Exhortation">Apostolic Exhortations</a> include <i>Ubi primum</i> (8 September 1914), <i>Allorché fummo chiamati</i> (28 July 1915) and <i>Dès le début</i> (1 August 1917). The <a href="/wiki/Papal_bulls" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal bulls">Papal bulls</a> of Benedict XV include <i>Incruentum Altaris</i> (10 August 1915), <i>Providentissima Mater</i> (27 May 1917), <i>Sedis huius</i> (14 May 1919), and <i>Divina disponente</i> (16 May 1920). Benedict issued nine <a href="/wiki/Breve" title="Breve">Briefs</a> during his pontificate: <i>Divinum praeceptum</i> (December 1915), <i>Romanorum Pontificum</i> (February 1916), <i>Cum Catholicae Ecclesiae</i> (April 1916), <i>Cum Biblia Sacra</i> (August 1916), <i>Cum Centesimus</i> (October 1916), <i>Centesimo Hodie</i> (October 1916), <i>Quod Ioannes</i> (April 1917), <i>In Africam quisnam</i> (June 1920), and <i>Quod nobis in condendo</i> (September 1920).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ad_beatissimi_Apostolorum"><i>Ad beatissimi Apostolorum</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Ad beatissimi Apostolorum">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><i><a href="/wiki/Ad_beatissimi_Apostolorum" title="Ad beatissimi Apostolorum">Ad beatissimi Apostolorum</a></i> is an <a href="/wiki/Encyclical" title="Encyclical">encyclical</a> of Benedict XV given at St. Peter's, Rome, on the Feast of All Saints on 1 November 1914, in the first year of his pontificate. This first encyclical coincided with the beginning of <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, which he labeled "The Suicide of Civilized Europe." Benedict described the combatants as the greatest and wealthiest nations of the earth, stating that "they are well-provided with the most awful weapons modern military science has devised, and they strive to destroy one another with refinements of horror. There is no limit to the measure of ruin and of slaughter; day by day the earth is drenched with newly shed blood and is covered with the bodies of the wounded and of the slain."<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In light of the senseless slaughter, the pope pleaded for "peace on earth to men of good will" (Luke 2:14), insisting that there are other ways and means whereby violated rights can be rectified.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The origin of the evil is a neglect of the precepts and practices of Christian wisdom, particularly a lack of love and compassion. Jesus Christ came down from Heaven for the very purpose of restoring among men the Kingdom of Peace, as He stated, "A new commandment I give unto you: That you love one another."<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> This message is repeated in John 15:12, in which Jesus says, "This is my commandment that you love one another."<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Materialism" title="Materialism">Materialism</a>, nationalism, racism, and <a href="/wiki/Class_conflict" title="Class conflict">class warfare</a> are the characteristics of the age instead, so Benedict XV described:
</p>
<ul><li>"Race hatred has reached its climax; peoples are more divided by jealousies than by frontiers; within one and the same nation, within the same city there rages the burning envy of class against class; and amongst individuals it is self-love which is the supreme law over-ruling everything."<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Humani_generis_redemptionem"><i>Humani generis redemptionem</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Humani generis redemptionem">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The encyclical <i><a href="/wiki/Humani_generis_redemptionem" title="Humani generis redemptionem">Humani generis redemptionem</a></i>, from 15 June 1917, deals with blatant ineffectiveness of Christian preaching. According to Benedict XV, there are more preachers of the Word than ever before, but "in the state of public and private morals as well as the constitutions and laws of nations, there is a general disregard and forgetfulness of the supernatural, a gradual falling away from the strict standard of Christian virtue, and that men are slipping back into the shameful practices of paganism."<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> The Pope squarely put part of the blame on those ministers of the Gospel who do not handle it as they should. It is not the times but the incompetent Christian preachers who are to blame, for no one today can say for sure that the Apostles were living in better times than ours. Perhaps, the encyclical states, that the Apostles found minds more readily devoted to the Gospel, or they may have met others with less opposition to the law of God.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As the encyclical tells, first are the Catholic bishops. The <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Trent" title="Council of Trent">Council of Trent</a> taught that preaching "is the paramount duty of Bishops."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The Apostles, whose successors the bishops are, looked upon the Church as something theirs, for it was they who received the grace of the Holy Spirit to begin it. Saint Paul wrote to the Corinthians, "Christ sent us not to baptize, but to preach the Gospel."<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Council of Trent Bishops are required to select for this priestly office those only who are "fit" for the position, i.e. those who "can exercise the ministry of preaching with profit to souls." Profiting souls does not mean doing such "eloquently or with popular applause, but rather with spiritual fruit."<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> The Pope requested that all the priests who are incapable of preaching or of hearing confession be removed from the position.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> The encyclical helps to draw out the message that priests must concentrate on the Word on God and the benefitting of souls before their own selves.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Quod_iam_diu"><i>Quod iam diu</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Quod iam diu">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><i><a href="/wiki/Quod_iam_diu" title="Quod iam diu">Quod iam diu</a></i> was an encyclical given at Rome at St. Peter's on 1 December 1918, in the fifth year of his Pontificate. It requested that, after <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, all Catholics of the world pray for a lasting peace and for those who are entrusted to make such during peace negotiations.
</p><p>The pope noted that true peace has not yet arrived, but the Armistice has suspended the slaughter and devastation by land, sea and air.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> It is the obligation of all Catholics to "invoke Divine assistance for all who take part in the peace conference," as the encyclical states. The Pope concludes that prayer is essential for the delegates who are to meet to define peace, as they are in need of much support.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Maximum_illud"><i>Maximum illud</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Maximum illud">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><i><a href="/wiki/Maximum_illud" title="Maximum illud">Maximum illud</a></i> is an <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_letter" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic letter">apostolic letter</a> of Benedict XV issued on 30 November 1919, dealing with the Catholic missions. After reminding bishops of their responsibility to support the missions, he advised missionaries not to regard the mission as their own but to welcome others to the task and to collaborate with those around them. He underlined the necessity of proper preparation for the work in foreign cultures and the need to acquire language skills before doing such work, especially in the Orient. He tells missionaries that: "Especially among the infidels, who are guided more by instinct than by reason, preaching by example is much more profitable than that of words". He requested a continued striving for personal sanctity and praised the selfless work of women religious in the missions.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> "Mission", however, "is not only for missionaries, but all Catholics must participate through their apostolate of prayer, by supporting vocations, and by helping financially."<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The letter concludes with the naming of several organizations which organize and supervise mission activities within the Catholic Church.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Internal_activities">Internal activities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Internal activities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonizations_and_beatifications">Canonizations and beatifications</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Canonizations and beatifications">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_people_beatified_by_Pope_Benedict_XV" title="List of people beatified by Pope Benedict XV">List of people beatified by Pope Benedict XV</a></div>
<p>Benedict XV canonized a total of four individuals including <a href="/wiki/Joan_of_Arc" title="Joan of Arc">Joan of Arc</a> and <a href="/wiki/Marguerite_Marie_Alacoque" class="mw-redirect" title="Marguerite Marie Alacoque">Marguerite Marie Alacoque</a>. He also beatified a total of forty six people, including the <a href="/wiki/Uganda_Martyrs" title="Uganda Martyrs">Uganda Martyrs</a> (1920), <a href="/wiki/Oliver_Plunkett" title="Oliver Plunkett">Oliver Plunkett</a> (1920) and <a href="/wiki/Louise_de_Marillac" title="Louise de Marillac">Louise de Marillac</a> (1920).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Doctor_of_the_Church">Doctor of the Church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Doctor of the Church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>He named <a href="/wiki/Ephrem_the_Syrian" title="Ephrem the Syrian">Ephrem the Syrian</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a> on 5 October 1920.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Consistories">Consistories</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Consistories">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Cardinals_created_by_Benedict_XV" title="Cardinals created by Benedict XV">Cardinals created by Benedict XV</a></div>
<p>The pope created 32 cardinals in five consistories, elevating men into the cardinalate such as <a href="/wiki/Pietro_La_Fontaine" title="Pietro La Fontaine">Pietro La Fontaine</a> (1916) and <a href="/wiki/Michael_von_Faulhaber" title="Michael von Faulhaber">Michael von Faulhaber</a> (1921); he reserved two <i><a href="/wiki/In_pectore" title="In pectore">in pectore</a></i> but later published one name (<a href="/wiki/Adolf_Bertram" title="Adolf Bertram">Adolf Bertram</a>). The pope's death in 1922 therefore invalidated the second appointment (it has been alleged that the second <i>in pectore</i> cardinal was to be <a href="/wiki/Pavel_Huyn" title="Pavel Huyn">Pavel Huyn</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Benedict XV also created his immediate successor <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Achille Ratti</a> as a cardinal in 1921.
</p><p>Benedict XV elevated 31 Europeans into the College of Cardinals with <a href="/wiki/Dennis_Joseph_Dougherty" title="Dennis Joseph Dougherty">Dennis Joseph Dougherty</a> as the single non-European cardinal appointed. In the 1916 consistory, no German or Austro-Hungarian cardinals were able to attend due to the intensity of the war. In that consistory, by naming Adolf Bertram as a cardinal "<i>in pectore</i>", Benedict XV hoped not to provoke any negativity in his selection from the Allies, particularly the Italians. Bertram was not formally invested in the Sacred College until December 1919, after the war had concluded.
</p><p>In the 1921 consistory, Benedict XV is reported to have said, that "We gave you the red robe of a Cardinal.... very soon, however, one of you will wear the white robe".<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personality_and_appearance">Personality and appearance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Personality and appearance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Pope Benedict XV was a slight man. He wore the smallest of three cassocks that were prepared for the election of a new pope in 1914, and became known as "Il Piccoletto" or "The Little Man". The cassock he wore upon his election had to be quickly stitched up so it could properly fit him. The new pope jokingly said to the tailors: "My dear, had you forgotten me?" He was dignified in bearing and courtly in terms of manners, but his appearance was not that of a pope. He had a sallow complexion, a mat of black hair, and prominent teeth.<sup id="cite_ref-Kertzer_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kertzer-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> He himself had referred to his appearance as an "ugly gargoyle upon the buildings of Rome". It was even said that his father looked upon his newborn son with incredulity and turned away in dismay at the sight of the infant della Chiesa, due to the small, bluish pallor and frail appearance of the infant.<sup id="cite_ref-Mag_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mag-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was renowned for his generosity, answering all pleas for help from poor Roman families with large cash gifts from his private revenues. When he was short on money, those who would be admitted to an audience would often be instructed by prelates not to mention their financial woes, as Benedict would inevitably feel guilty that he could not help the needy at the time. He also depleted the Vatican's official revenues with large-scale charitable expenditure during World War I. Upon his death, the Vatican Treasury had been depleted to the equivalent in Italian lire of U.S. $19,000 ($307,600 in 2023).<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Benedict XV was a careful innovator by Vatican standards. He was known to carefully consider all novelties before he ordered their implementation, then insisting on them to the fullest. He rejected clinging to the past for the past's sake with the words "Let us live in the present and not in history."<sup id="cite_ref-De_Waal_122_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-De_Waal_122-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> His relation to secular Italian powers was reserved yet positive, avoiding conflict and tacitly supporting the Royal Family of Italy. Yet, like <a href="/wiki/Pius_IX" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius IX">Pius IX</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leo_XIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Leo XIII">Leo XIII</a>, he also protested against interventions of State authorities in internal Church affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-De_Waal_122_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-De_Waal_122-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Pope Benedict was not considered a man of letters. He did not publish educational or devotional books. His encyclicals are pragmatic and down-to-earth, intelligent and at times far-sighted. He remained neutral during the battles of the "Great War," when almost everybody else was claiming "sides." Like that of <a href="/wiki/Pius_XII" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius XII">Pius XII</a> during World War II, his neutrality was questioned by all sides then and even to this day.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Benedict XV personally had a strong devotion to the <a href="/wiki/Blessed_Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Blessed Virgin Mary">Blessed Virgin Mary</a>. He gave his approval to the dioceses of Belgium for a Mass and office under the title of Mary as Mediatrix of All Graces.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:187px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Benedict_XV_statue.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Pope_Benedict_XV_statue.jpg/185px-Pope_Benedict_XV_statue.jpg" decoding="async" width="185" height="247" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Pope_Benedict_XV_statue.jpg/278px-Pope_Benedict_XV_statue.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Pope_Benedict_XV_statue.jpg/370px-Pope_Benedict_XV_statue.jpg 2x" data-file-width="480" data-file-height="640" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Benedict_XV_statue.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of Benedict XV in the courtyard of <a href="/wiki/St._Esprit_Cathedral,_Istanbul" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Esprit Cathedral, Istanbul">St. Esprit Cathedral</a>, Istanbul</div></div></div>
<p>Benedict XV celebrated Mass with the nuns at the <a href="/wiki/Domus_Sanctae_Marthae" title="Domus Sanctae Marthae">Domus Sanctae Marthae</a> in early January 1922 and while he waited for his driver out in the rain he fell ill with the flu which turned into <a href="/wiki/Pneumonia" title="Pneumonia">pneumonia</a>. The pope gave evidence on 5 January that he was suffering from a cold, but it was later noted on 12 January that he was suffering from a heavy cough and was feverish. On 18 January, the pope was unable to get out of bed.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> The pope's condition grew grave on 19 January around 11:00pm after his heart grew weak due to the spreading of pneumonia, with the Holy See notifying the Italian government that the pope's condition was grave. <a href="/wiki/Oxygen" title="Oxygen">Oxygen</a> was administered to the pope after respiration became increasingly difficult, and Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Oreste_Giorgi" title="Oreste Giorgi">Oreste Giorgi</a> was summoned to the pontiff's bedside to recite prayers for the dying. His condition improved slightly at midnight on 20 January, and he insisted his medical attendants withdraw for the night when it seemed he might recover.
</p><p>At 2:00&#160;am on 21 January, he was given the <a href="/wiki/Extreme_Unction" class="mw-redirect" title="Extreme Unction">Extreme Unction</a> and the pope took some liquid refreshment following an hour's sleep. It was at some stage around this time that he met privately with Cardinal Gasparri for around 20 minutes to communicate his last wishes to the cardinal, while entrusting his last will to him. A bulletin at 4:30&#160;am indicated that the pope's speech was occasionally incoherent while another at 9:55&#160;am indicated that the pope's agony was profound to the point he could not recognize his attendants due to his state of delirium. Another bulletin at 10:05&#160;am said that the pope's pulse was becoming intermittent.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> At noon, he grew delirious and insisted on rising to resume his work, but an hour later fell into a <a href="/wiki/Coma" title="Coma">coma</a>. By 12:30pm, <a href="/wiki/Ludovico_Chigi_Albani_della_Rovere" title="Ludovico Chigi Albani della Rovere">Prince Chigi-Albani</a> visited the pope's room to prepare to take possession of the apartment in the event of the pope's death and to act as a marshal for the next conclave.
</p><p>False reports from Parisian and London evening papers on 21 January announced the pope's death occurring at 5:00&#160;am that day, warranting corrections by Italian correspondents, prior to an official dispatch at 8:00&#160;am to inform that the pope was alive.<sup id="cite_ref-Trove_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trove-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Francis_Bourne" title="Francis Bourne">Bourne</a>'s secretary was also forced to announce on 21 January that the pope had not died after a member of the cardinal's staff mistakenly confirmed the pope had died.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The death throes began at 5:20&#160;am on 22 January, with Cardinal Giorgi granting absolution to the dying pope. Cardinal Gasparri arrived at Benedict XV's bedside at 5:30&#160;am since the pope had fallen into a coma yet again (at 5:18&#160;am he said that "the catastrophe is imminent"), with Doctor Cherubini pronouncing the pontiff's death at 6:00&#160;am.<sup id="cite_ref-Trove_100-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trove-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> After his death, flags were flown at <a href="/wiki/Half-staff" class="mw-redirect" title="Half-staff">half-staff</a> in memory of him and as a tribute to him. His body then lay in state for the people to see before being moved for burial in the Vatican grottos.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Benedict_XV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Benedict XV made valiant efforts to end World War I. In 2005, Pope <a href="/wiki/Benedict_XVI" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedict XVI">Benedict XVI</a> recognized the significance of his long-ago predecessor's commitment to peace by taking the same name upon his own rise to the pontificate. Benedict XV's humane approach to the world in 1914–1918 starkly contrasted with most of the monarchs and leaders of the time, with few notable exceptions. His worth is reflected in the tribute engraved at the foot of the statue that the <a href="/wiki/Turkic_peoples" title="Turkic peoples">Turks</a>, a non-Catholic, non-Christian people, erected of him in <a href="/wiki/Istanbul" title="Istanbul">Istanbul</a>: "The great Pope of the world tragedy,... the benefactor of all people, irrespective of nationality or religion." This monument stands in the courtyard of the <a href="/wiki/St._Esprit_Cathedral,_Istanbul" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Esprit Cathedral, Istanbul">St. Esprit Cathedral</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> showed high regard for Benedict XV, who had consecrated him a bishop on 13 May 1917, the very day of the first reported <a href="/wiki/Vision_(spirituality)" title="Vision (spirituality)">apparitions</a> of <a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Fatima" class="mw-redirect" title="Our Lady of Fatima">Our Lady of Fatima</a>. While Pius XII considered another Benedict, <a href="/wiki/Benedict_XIV" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedict XIV">Benedict XIV</a>, in terms of his sanctity and scholarly contributions to be worthy as <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> he thought that Benedict XV during his short pontificate was truly a man of God, who worked for peace.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> He helped prisoners of war and many others who needed help in dire times, and was extremely generous to Russia.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> He praised him as a Marian Pope who promoted the devotion to <a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Lourdes" title="Our Lady of Lourdes">Our Lady of Lourdes</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> for his encyclicals <i><a href="/wiki/Ad_beatissimi_Apostolorum" title="Ad beatissimi Apostolorum">Ad beatissimi Apostolorum</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Humani_generis_redemptionem" title="Humani generis redemptionem">Humani generis redemptionem</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Quod_iam_diu" title="Quod iam diu">Quod iam diu</a></i>, and <i>Spiritus Paraclitus</i>, and for the codification of <a href="/wiki/Canon_Law" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon Law">Canon Law</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> in which, under della Chiesa and Pietro Gasparri, Pius XII had had, as Eugenio Pacelli, the opportunity to participate.
</p><p>Pope Benedict XVI showed his own admiration for Benedict XV following his election to the papacy on 19 April 2005. The election of a new Pope is often accompanied by conjecture over his choice of papal name; it is widely believed that a Pope chooses the name of a predecessor whose teachings and legacy he wishes to continue. Cardinal Ratzinger's choice of "Benedict" was seen as a signal that Benedict XV's views on humanitarian diplomacy and his stance against <a href="/wiki/Relativism" title="Relativism">relativism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Modernism_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Modernism in the Catholic Church">modernism</a> would be emulated during the reign of the new Pope. During his first <a href="/wiki/Audience_(meeting)" title="Audience (meeting)">General Audience</a> in <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Square" title="St. Peter&#39;s Square">St. Peter's Square</a> on 27 April 2005, Pope Benedict XVI paid tribute to Benedict XV when explaining his choice:<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>"Filled with sentiments of awe and thanksgiving, I wish to speak of why I chose the name Benedict. Firstly, I remember Pope Benedict XV, that courageous prophet of peace, who guided the Church through turbulent times of war. In his footsteps I place my ministry in the service of reconciliation and harmony between peoples."</p></blockquote>

<li id="cite_note-25"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-25">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Note on numbering: <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Benedict_X" title="Antipope Benedict X">Pope Benedict X</a> is now considered an <a href="/wiki/Antipope" title="Antipope">antipope</a>. At the time, however, this status was not recognized, and so the man the Catholic Church officially considers the <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XI" title="Pope Benedict XI">tenth true Pope Benedict</a> took the official number XI, rather than X. This has advanced the numbering of all subsequent Popes Benedict by one. Popes Benedict XI-XVI are, from an official point of view, the tenth through fifteenth popes by that name. In other words, there is no legitimate Pope Benedict X.</span>
</li>
